  /testbed/sepal_ui/scripts/gee.py:10: DeprecationWarning: Call to deprecated function (or staticmethod) need_ee. (use sepal_ui.scripts.decorator.need_ee instead<#sep#>from torch.nn import Module
import torch
from torch import nn
from torch.nn import functional as F
from torch.nn import Conv2d
from torch.nn import ReLU
from torch.nn.modules.utils import _pair


class DropBlock2D(object):

    def __init__(self, *args, **kwargs):
        raise NotImplementedError


class rSoftMax(nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, radix, cardinality):
        super().__init__()
        self.radix = radix
        self.cardinality = cardinality

    def forward(self, x):
        batch = x.size(0)
        if self.radix > 1:
            x = x.view(batch, self.cardinality, self.radix, -1).transpose(1, 2)
            x = F.softmax(x, dim=1)
            x = x.reshape(batch, -1)
        else:
            x = torch.sigmoid(x)
        return x


class SplAtConv2d(Module):
    """Split-Attention Conv2d
    """

    def __init__(self, in_channels, channels, kernel_size, stride=(1, 1),
        padding=(0, 0), dilation=(1, 1), groups=1, bias=True, radix=2,
        reduction_factor=4, norm_layer=None, dropblock_prob=0.0, **kwargs):
        super(SplAtConv2d, self).__init__()
        padding = _pair(padding)
        inter_channels = max(in_channels * radix // reduction_factor, 32)
        self.radix = radix
        self.cardinality = groups
        self.channels = channels
        self.dropblock_prob = dropblock_prob
        self.conv = Conv2d(in_channels, channels * radix, kernel_size,
            stride, padding, dilation, groups=groups * radix, bias=bias, **
            kwargs)
        self.use_bn = norm_layer is not None
        if self.use_bn:
            self.bn0 = norm_layer(channels * radix)
        self.relu = ReLU(inplace=True)
        self.fc1 = Conv2d(channels, inter_channels, 1, groups=self.cardinality)
        if self.use_bn:
            self.bn1 = norm_layer(inter_channels)
        self.fc2 = Conv2d(inter_channels, channels * radix, 1, groups=self.
            cardinality)
        if dropblock_prob > 0.0:
            self.dropblock = DropBlock2D(dropblock_prob, 3)
        self.rsoftmax = rSoftMax(radix, groups)

    def forward(self, x):
        x = self.conv(x)
        if self.use_bn:
            x = self.bn0(x)
        if self.dropblock_prob > 0.0:
            x = self.dropblock(x)
        x = self.relu(x)
        batch, rchannel = x.shape[:2]
        if self.radix > 1:
            if torch.__version__ < '1.5':
                splited = torch.split(x, int(rchannel // self.radix), dim=1)
            else:
                splited = torch.split(x, rchannel // self.radix, dim=1)
            gap = sum(splited)
        else:
            gap = x
        gap = F.adaptive_avg_pool2d(gap, 1)
        gap = self.fc1(gap)
        if self.use_bn:
            gap = self.bn1(gap)
        gap = self.relu(gap)
        atten = self.fc2(gap)
        atten = self.rsoftmax(atten).view(batch, -1, 1, 1)
        if self.radix > 1:
            if torch.__version__ < '1.5':
                attens = torch.split(atten, int(rchannel // self.radix), dim=1)
            else:
                attens = torch.split(atten, rchannel // self.radix, dim=1)
            out = sum([(att * split) for att, split in zip(attens, splited)])
        else:
            out = atten * x
        return out.contiguous()


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'in_channels': 4, 'channels': 4, 'kernel_size': 4}]
<#sep#> However, f(7) and f(24) are part of other equations. For instance, let's check if f(73)=8f(8)+3f(3)=8*8k +3*3k=64k +9k=73k=73*1=73. But if we use the modified f(7)=31 and f(24)=17, does this affect other values? Let's see. For example, 24 is part of f(625)=24f(24) +7f(7)=24*17 +7*31=408 +217=625. Which is correct since f(625)=625k=625*1=625. So that's consistent. Similarly, f(15)=15k=15*1=15. So even with t=1, these equations hold. But then, what about f(15)=15k=15. Is there a way to check if f(15) must be 15k? For example, 15 can't be written as sum of two squares, but it's part of f(225)=15f(15). Also, 225 can be written as 9^2 +12^2. Let's check: 9^2 +12^2=81+144=225. Therefore, f(225)=9f(9) +12f(12)=9*(9k) +12f(12). But f(225)=15f(15)=15*(15k)=225k. So 81k +12f(12)=225k ⇒12f(12)=144k ⇒f(12)=12k. So f(12)=12k. But if k=1, f(12)=12. How do we determine f(12)? From another decomposition. 12 can be written as 2^2 + 2*sqrt(2)^2, but sqrt(2) isn't integer. So only 0^2 +sqrt(12)^2, which isn't integer. So f(12) is not directly constrained by the equation. However, f(12) can be determined through f(12^2)=12f(12). But 144=12^2. So f(144)=12f(12). Also, 144 can be written as other sums of squares. For example, 144=12^2 +0^2, same as before. Or 144= (sqrt(144))^2 +0^2. No other integer decompositions. Therefore, f(144)=12f(12). But if we set k=1 and t=1, then f(12)=12*1=12, which is consistent. But if we had set f(12)= something else, would that be allowed? Let's see. Suppose f(12)=12k +m. Then f(144)=12*(12k +m)=144k +12m. But 144 is also 0^2 +12^2, so f(144)=12f(12)=144k +12m. But if we also have f(144)=12f(12). So no, it's consistent. Therefore, even if we set f(12)=12k +m, we get f(144)=12*(12k +m). But since f(144)=144k, this implies 144k=144k +12m ⇒12m=0 ⇒m=0. Therefore, f(12)=12k. So even though 12 can't be written as sum of two squares, f(12) is forced to be 12k. Therefore, any deviation in f(12) would violate the equation for f(144). 

This suggests that all numbers, even those not expressible as sums of two squares, must be multiples of k. Let's formalize this. Suppose we let f(1)=k. Then, using the equation and the relations we've derived, f(n) must be nk for all n. Let's prove this by induction. 

Base case: n=0. f(0)=0=0*k. 

Assume f(m) = m k for all m < n. Now, consider n. If n can be written as a sum of two squares, say n=x^2 +y^2, then f(n)=x f(x) + y f(y)=x (x k) + y (y k)=k(x^2 + y^2)=k n. 

If n cannot be written as a sum of two squares, then there might not be such x and y. However, for numbers that cannot be written as sums of two squares, their squares n^2 will require f(n^2)=n f(n). But n^2 can also be written as n^2 +0^2, so f(n^2)=n f(n) +0= n f(n). But also, since n^2 is a square, the square relation gives f(n^2)=n f(n). Therefore, this doesn't add new information. However, even if n can't be written as sum of two squares, n^2 can be used in other equations. For example, suppose we take m =n^2 + y^2 for some y. Then f(m)=n f(n) + y f(y). But if m can be written as a sum of squares in another way, then that equation must hold. But this might not help unless m can be written in multiple ways. 

Alternatively, let's consider that for any number m that is a square, f(m)=sqrt(m) f(sqrt(m)). So recursively, if we can show that for all numbers up to m, they are multiples of k, then f(m)=sqrt(m) f(sqrt(m)) implies that f(m)=sqrt(m)*sqrt(m) k=m k. 

Wait, but sqrt(m) must be an integer because m is a square. Let's say m = p^2. Then f(p^2)=p f(p). If by induction hypothesis, f(p)=p k, then f(p^2)=p*(p k)=p^2 k=m k. Therefore, for squares, this holds. 

But for non-squares, how do we ensure they are multiples of k? If a non-square n is such that there exists some decomposition involving n in another equation. For example, if we can find a decomposition of some larger number that includes n, then n must be a multiple of k. 

But perhaps a better approach is to note that for any n, if we can write n^2 =x^2 +y^2, then n^2 =x^2 + y^2. But n^2 can only be written as x^2 + y^2 in certain ways. For example, if n is a hypotenuse of a Pythagorean triple, but not necessarily. 

Alternatively, let's take n^2 +0^2 =n^2. So f(n^2)=n f(n). If we can write n^2 as another sum of squares, like a^2 +b^2, then f(n^2)=a f(a)+b f(b). But if n^2 is written as a^2 +b^2, then a and b must satisfy a^2 +b^2 =n^2. So this is a Pythagorean triple. Therefore, if n is part of a Pythagorean triple, then f(n^2)=a f(a) +b f(b). But since f(n^2)=n f(n), we have n f(n)=a f(a)+b f(b). If a and b are less than n, then by induction hypothesis, f(a)=a k and f(b)=b k. Therefore, n f(n)=a^2 k +b^2 k= (a^2 +b^2)k =n^2 k. Therefore, n f(n)=n^2 k ⇒f(n)=n k. 

Therefore, if n is the hypotenuse of a Pythagorean triple, then f(n)=n k. However, not all numbers are hypotenuses of Pythagorean triples. For example, 1, 2, 3, etc. However, for numbers that are not hypotenuses, how do we ensure f(n)=n k? 

But wait, 3 is not a hypotenuse, but we previously saw that f(3)=3k because 3 is part of the equation for f(25)=3 f(3) +4 f(4). And since f(25)=25k and f(4)=4k, this forces f(3)=3k. Similarly, numbers that are not hypotenuses can still be involved in other equations. 

Therefore, perhaps every number can be shown to satisfy f(n)=n k by induction. Let's formalize this.

**Induction Hypothesis**: For all m < n, f(m) =m k.

**Base Case**:<#sep#>human: After making a strategic plan with carriers for expansion of mobile network throughout the whole country, the government decided to cover rural areas with the last generation of 5G network.

Since 5G antenna towers will be built in the area of mainly private properties, the government needs an easy way to find information about landowners for each property partially or fully contained in the planned building area. 

The planned building area is represented as a rectangle with sides width and height.

Every 5G antenna tower occupies a circle with a center in (x,y) and radius r. 

There is a database of Geodetic Institute containing information about each property. Each property is defined with its identification number and polygon represented as an array of (x,y) points in the counter-clockwise direction. 

Your task is to build an IT system which can handle queries of type (x, y, r) in which (x,y) represents a circle center, while r represents its radius. The IT system should return the total area of properties that need to be acquired for the building of a tower so that the government can estimate the price. Furthermore, the system should return a list of identification numbers of these properties (so that the owners can be contacted for land acquisition).

A property needs to be acquired if the circle of the antenna tower is intersecting or touching it.
Input

The first line contains the size of the building area as double values width, height, and an integer n — the number of properties in the database. 

Each of the next n lines contains the description of a single property in the form of an integer number v (3 ≤ v ≤ 40) — the number of points that define a property, as well as 2*v double numbers — the coordinates (x,y) of each property point. Line i (0 ≤ i ≤ n-1) contains the information for property with id i.

The next line contains an integer q — the number of queries. 

Each of the next q lines contains double values x, y, r — the coordinates of an antenna circle center (x, y) and its radius r.

1 ≤ n * q ≤ 10^6

Output

For each of the q queries, your program should output a line containing the total area of all the properties that need to be acquired, an integer representing the number of such properties, as well as the list of ids of these properties (separated by blank characters, arbitrary order).

Example

Input


10 10 3
4 2 2 3 2 3 3 2 3
3 3.5 2 4.5 2 4.5 3
4 7 8 7.5 8.5 8 8 7.5 9
5
2 3.5 0.5
3.3 2 0.4
5 2.5 0.5
7.5 8.5 0.5
3 7 0.5


Output


1.000000 1 0 
1.500000 2 0 1 
0.500000 1 1 
0.250000 1 2 
0.000000 0 

Note

You can assume that the land not covered with properties (polygons) is under the government's ownership and therefore doesn't need to be acquired. Properties do not intersect with each other.

Precision being used for solution checking is 10^{-4}.

tests/test_SepalMap.py<#sep#>import math
import numpy
import torch
import numpy as np
import torch.nn as nn
import torch.utils.model_zoo
import torch as th
import numpy.matlib


def conv_nd(dims, *args, **kwargs):
    """
    Create a 1D, 2D, or 3D convolution module.
    """
    if dims == 1:
        return nn.Conv1d(*args, **kwargs)
    elif dims == 2:
        return nn.Conv2d(*args, **kwargs)
    elif dims == 3:
        return nn.Conv3d(*args, **kwargs)
    raise ValueError(f'unsupported dimensions: {dims}')


def count_flops_attn(model, _x, y):
    """
    A counter for the `thop` package to count the operations in an
    attention operation.
    Meant to be used like:
        macs, params = thop.profile(
            model,
            inputs=(inputs, timestamps),
            custom_ops={QKVAttention: QKVAttention.count_flops},
        )
    """
    b, c, *spatial = y[0].shape
    num_spatial = int(np.prod(spatial))
    matmul_ops = 2 * b * num_spatial ** 2 * c
    model.total_ops += th.DoubleTensor([matmul_ops])


class QKVAttention(nn.Module):
    """
    A module which performs QKV attention and splits in a different order.
    """

    def __init__(self, n_heads):
        super().__init__()
        self.n_heads = n_heads

    def forward(self, qkv):
        """
        Apply QKV attention.

        :param qkv: an [N x (3 * H * C) x T] tensor of Qs, Ks, and Vs.
        :return: an [N x (H * C) x T] tensor after attention.
        """
        bs, width, length = qkv.shape
        assert width % (3 * self.n_heads) == 0
        ch = width // (3 * self.n_heads)
        q, k, v = qkv.chunk(3, dim=1)
        scale = 1 / math.sqrt(math.sqrt(ch))
        weight = th.einsum('bct,bcs->bts', (q * scale).view(bs * self.
            n_heads, ch, length), (k * scale).view(bs * self.n_heads, ch,
            length))
        weight = th.softmax(weight.float(), dim=-1).type(weight.dtype)
        a = th.einsum('bts,bcs->bct', weight, v.reshape(bs * self.n_heads,
            ch, length))
        return a.reshape(bs, -1, length)

    @staticmethod
    def count_flops(model, _x, y):
        return count_flops_attn(model, _x, y)


class AttentionPool2d(nn.Module):
    """
    Adapted from CLIP: https://github.com/openai/CLIP/blob/main/clip/model.py
    """

    def __init__(self, spacial_dim: 'int', embed_dim: 'int',
        num_heads_channels: 'int', output_dim: 'int'=None):
        super().__init__()
        self.positional_embedding = nn.Parameter(th.randn(embed_dim, 
            spacial_dim ** 2 + 1) / embed_dim ** 0.5)
        self.qkv_proj = conv_nd(1, embed_dim, 3 * embed_dim, 1)
        self.c_proj = conv_nd(1, embed_dim, output_dim or embed_dim, 1)
        self.num_heads = embed_dim // num_heads_channels
        self.attention = QKVAttention(self.num_heads)

    def forward(self, x):
        b, c, *_spatial = x.shape
        x = x.reshape(b, c, -1)
        x = th.cat([x.mean(dim=-1, keepdim=True), x], dim=-1)
        x = x + self.positional_embedding[None, :, :]
        x = self.qkv_proj(x)
        x = self.attention(x)
        x = self.c_proj(x)
        return x[:, :, 0]


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'spacial_dim': 4, 'embed_dim': 4, 'num_heads_channels': 4}]
<#sep#>use, but we previously saw that f(3)=3k because 3 is part of the equation for f(25)=3 f(3) +4 f(4). And since f(25)=25k and f(4)=4k, this forces f(3)=3k. Similarly, numbers that are not hypotenuses can still be involved in other equations. 

Therefore, perhaps every number can be shown to satisfy f(n)=n k by induction. Let's formalize this.

**Induction Hypothesis**: For all m < n, f(m) =m k.

**Base Case**: m=0, f(0)=0=0*k.

**Inductive Step**: Suppose for all m <n, f(m)=m k. We need to show f(n)=n k.

If n can be written as a sum of two squares, say n=x^2 +y^2, then f(n)=x f(x) +y f(y). Since x and y are less than or equal to n (since x^2 <=n, so x<=sqrt(n) <n if n>=1). Wait, not necessarily. For example, if n=2, x and y can be 1 and1. So x and y can be less than n. But if n=5, x=2, y=1, which are less than 5. But for larger n, x and y could be larger? No, because x^2 + y^2 =n, so both x and y must be <= sqrt(n). If n is a sum of squares, then x and y <=sqrt(n). If n >=1, sqrt(n) <=n, with equality only if n=0 or 1. So for n >=2, sqrt(n) <n. Therefore, x and y are less than n. Therefore, by induction hypothesis, f(x)=x k and f(y)=y k. Therefore, f(n)=x(x k) +y(y k)=k(x^2 +y^2)=k n. Therefore, if n can be written as a sum of two squares, then f(n)=n k.

If n cannot be written as a sum of two squares, consider n^2. Then f(n^2)=n f(n). But n^2 can be written as a sum of squares in the form n^2 =n^2 +0^2. Therefore, f(n^2)=n f(n). But also, n^2 can potentially be written as a sum of two squares in another way. For example, 25=3^2 +4^2. If n^2 can be written as a sum of two squares a^2 +b^2 where a and b are less than n, then f(n^2)=a f(a) +b f(b)=a(a k)+b(b k)=k(a^2 +b^2)=k n^2. But since f(n^2)=n f(n), this gives n f(n)=k n^2 ⇒f(n)=k n.

But what if n^2 cannot be written as a sum of two squares except trivially as n^2 +0^2? For example, take n=7. 7^2=49=7^2 +0^2. There are no other decompositions of 49 as sum of two squares. Therefore, f(49)=7 f(7). But 49 can also be part of other equations. For example, 24^2 +7^2=576 +49=625=25^2. So f(625)=24f(24) +7f(7). But f(625)=25 f(25)=25*(25k)=625k. From the induction hypothesis, since 24 <25, f(24)=24k. So substituting, 24*24k +7 f(7)=625k ⇒576k +7f(7)=625k ⇒7f(7)=49k ⇒f(7)=7k. Therefore, even though 7 cannot be written as sum of two squares, and 7^2 can't be written as sum of two squares non-trivially, the function f(7) is determined through another equation involving a larger number where 7 is a component.

Therefore, by induction, for any n, even if it can't be written as a sum of two squares, its value f(n) is determined by the equation involving a larger number where n is a component. This ensures that f(n) must be n k.

Hence, by mathematical induction, the only solutions are linear functions f(n) =k n for some non-negative integer k. 

To confirm, let's check the zero function. If k=0, then f(n)=0 for all n. Plugging into the equation: f(x^2 +y^2)=0, and x f(x)+y f(y)=0+0=0. So it works.

For any other k, f(n)=k n works as shown earlier.

Therefore, the solutions are all functions f(n) =k n where k is a non-negative integer.
</think>To find all functions \( f: \mathbb{N}_0 \to \mathbb{N}_0 \) that satisfy the equation \( f(x^2 + y^2) = x f(x) + y f(y) \), we proceed as follows:

1. **Initial Observations:**
   - Let \( x = 0 \). The equation becomes \( f(y^2) = y f(y) \).
   - Let \( y = 0 \). The equation becomes \( f(x^2) = x f(x) \).
   - Therefore, for any \( a \in \mathbb{N}_0 \), we have \( f(a^2) = a f(a) \).

2. **Testing Simple Values:**
   - For \( x = 1 \) and \( y = 0 \), we get \( f(1) = 1 \cdot f(1) + 0 \cdot f(0) \), which is trivially true.
   - For \( x = 1 \) and \( y = 1 \), we get \( f(2) = 1 \cdot f(1) + 1 \cdot f(1) = 2 f(1) \). Let \( f(1) = c \), then \( f(2) = 2c \).

3. **General Form Hypothesis:**
   - Suppose \( f(n) = k n \) for some constant \( k \). Substituting into the original equation:
     \[
     f(x^2 + y^2) = k(x^2 + y^2)
     \]
     \[
     x f(x) + y f(y) = x(kx) + y(ky) = kx^2 + ky^2 = k(x^2 + y^2)
     \]
     Both sides are equal, so \( f(n) = k n \) is a solution.

4. **Checking for Other Possible Solutions:**
   - Consider \( f(n) = 0 \) for all \( n \). Substituting into the original equation:
     \[
     f(x^2 + y^2) = 0
     \]
     \[
     x f(x) + y f(y) = x \cdot 0 + y \cdot 0 = 0
     \]
     Both sides are equal, so \( f(n) = 0 \) is also a solution, which is the case when \( k = 0 \).

5. **Uniqueness of Linear Solutions:**
   - Suppose there exists a function \( f \) that is not of the form \( f(n) = k n \). Consider \( f(0) = 0 \) and \( f(1) = c \). From the equation \( f(1) = c \), we have \( f(2) = 2c \).
   - Assume \( f(n) = k n \) for all \( n \) up to some \( m \). For \( n = m+1 \), if \( m+1 \) can be written as \( x^2 + y^2 \), then:
     \[
     f(m+1) = x f(x) + y f(y) = x(kx) + y(ky) = k(x^2 + y^2) = k(m+1)
     \]
     If \( m+1 \) cannot be written as \( x^2 + y^2 \), consider \( (m+1)^2 \):
     \[
     f((m+1)^2) = (m+1) f(m+1)
     \]
     Since \( (m+1)^2 \) can be written as \( (m+1)^2 + 0^2 \), we have<#sep#> accumulated, their areas summed, and their IDs collected.

For a polygon, the area can be computed using the shoelace formula, since the polygons are given in counter-clockwise (ordered). import torch.nn.functional
import torch.jit
import torch.nn.functional as F
from functools import partial
from torch.nn.modules.loss import _Loss


def reduced_focal_loss(outputs: 'torch.Tensor', targets: 'torch.Tensor',
    threshold: 'float'=0.5, gamma: 'float'=2.0, reduction='mean'):
    """
    Compute reduced focal loss between target and output logits.
    Source https://github.com/BloodAxe/pytorch-toolbelt
    See :class:`~pytorch_toolbelt.losses` for details.
    Args:
        outputs: Tensor of arbitrary shape
        targets: Tensor of the same shape as input
        reduction (string, optional):
            Specifies the reduction to apply to the output:
            "none" | "mean" | "sum" | "batchwise_mean".
            "none": no reduction will be applied,
            "mean": the sum of the output will be divided by the number of
            elements in the output,
            "sum": the output will be summed.
            Note: :attr:`size_average` and :attr:`reduce`
            are in the process of being deprecated,
            and in the meantime, specifying either of those two args
            will override :attr:`reduction`.
            "batchwise_mean" computes mean loss per sample in batch.
            Default: "mean"
    See https://arxiv.org/abs/1903.01347
    """
    targets = targets.type(outputs.type())
    logpt = -F.binary_cross_entropy_with_logits(outputs, targets, reduction
        ='none')
    pt = torch.exp(logpt)
    focal_reduction = ((1.0 - pt) / threshold).pow(gamma)
    focal_reduction[pt < threshold] = 1
    loss = -focal_reduction * logpt
    if reduction == 'mean':
        loss = loss.mean()
    if reduction == 'sum':
        loss = loss.sum()
    if reduction == 'batchwise_mean':
        loss = loss.sum(0)
    return loss


def sigmoid_focal_loss(outputs: 'torch.Tensor', targets: 'torch.Tensor',
    gamma: 'float'=2.0, alpha: 'float'=0.25, reduction: 'str'='mean'):
    """
    Compute binary focal loss between target and output logits.
    Source https://github.com/BloodAxe/pytorch-toolbelt
    See :class:`~pytorch_toolbelt.losses` for details.
    Args:
        outputs: Tensor of arbitrary shape
        targets: Tensor of the same shape as input
        reduction (string, optional):
            Specifies the reduction to apply to the output:
            "none" | "mean" | "sum" | "batchwise_mean".
            "none": no reduction will be applied,
            "mean": the sum of the output will be divided by the number of
            elements in the output,
            "sum": the output will be summed.
    See https://github.com/open-mmlab/mmdetection/blob/master/mmdet/core/loss/losses.py  # noqa: E501
    """
    targets = targets.type(outputs.type())
    logpt = -F.binary_cross_entropy_with_logits(outputs, targets, reduction
        ='none')
    pt = torch.exp(logpt)
    loss = -(1 - pt).pow(gamma) * logpt
    if alpha is not None:
        loss = loss * (alpha * targets + (1 - alpha) * (1 - targets))
    if reduction == 'mean':
        loss = loss.mean()
    if reduction == 'sum':
        loss = loss.sum()
    if reduction == 'batchwise_mean':
        loss = loss.sum(0)
    return loss


class FocalLossBinary(_Loss):

    def __init__(self, ignore: 'int'=None, reduced: 'bool'=False, gamma:
        'float'=2.0, alpha: 'float'=0.25, threshold: 'float'=0.5, reduction:
        'str'='mean'):
        """
        Compute focal loss for binary classification problem.
        """
        super().__init__()
        self.ignore = ignore
        if reduced:
            self.loss_fn = partial(reduced_focal_loss, gamma=gamma,
                threshold=threshold, reduction=reduction)
        else:
            self.loss_fn = partial(sigmoid_focal_loss, gamma=gamma, alpha=
                alpha, reduction=reduction)

    def forward(self, logits, targets):
        """
        Args:
            logits: [bs; ...]
            targets: [bs; ...]
        """
        targets = targets.view(-1)
        logits = logits.view(-1)
        if self.ignore is not None:
            not_ignored = targets != self.ignore
            logits = logits[not_ignored]
            targets = targets[not_ignored]
        loss = self.loss_fn(logits, targets)
        return loss


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {}]

The algorithm for finding if a line segment intersects a circle:

Compute the vector from P to Q: v = Q - P.

The direction vector is v normalized, but can keep as is (not normalized).

The point on the segment can be parametrized as P + t*v where t ranges from 0 to 1.

(xc - (px + t*(qx-px)))^2 + (yc - (py + t*(qy -py)))^2 = r^2

Expand this equation, resulting in a quadratic equation in terms of t: a t^2 + b t + c =0.

Solve for t. 

If the quadratic equation has a real solution, then there are points on the line where the distance is exactly r. 

We then check if any of these solutions lie within 0 <= t <=1. But also, since the equation is a quadratic, we can compute discriminant. 

Alternatively, if the discriminant is negative, no real solutions. Then the line does not intersect the circle at all.

  <#sep#>import torch
from torchvision.transforms import *


class ConvBlock(torch.nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, input_size, output_size, kernel_size=3, stride=1,
        padding=1, bias=True, activation='prelu', norm=None):
        super(ConvBlock, self).__init__()
        self.conv = torch.nn.Conv2d(input_size, output_size, kernel_size,
            stride, padding, bias=bias)
        self.norm = norm
        if self.norm == 'batch':
            self.bn = torch.nn.BatchNorm2d(output_size)
        elif self.norm == 'instance':
            self.bn = torch.nn.InstanceNorm2d(output_size)
        self.activation = activation
        if self.activation == 'relu':
            self.act = torch.nn.ReLU(True)
        elif self.activation == 'prelu':
            self.act = torch.nn.PReLU()
        elif self.activation == 'lrelu':
            self.act = torch.nn.LeakyReLU(0.2, True)
        elif self.activation == 'tanh':
            self.act = torch.nn.Tanh()
        elif self.activation == 'sigmoid':
            self.act = torch.nn.Sigmoid()

    def forward(self, x):
        if self.norm is not None:
            out = self.bn(self.conv(x))
        else:
            out = self.conv(x)
        if self.activation is not None:
            return self.act(out)
        else:
            return out


class DeconvBlock(torch.nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, input_size, output_size, kernel_size=4, stride=2,
        padding=1, bias=True, activation='prelu', norm=None):
        super(DeconvBlock, self).__init__()
        self.deconv = torch.nn.ConvTranspose2d(input_size, output_size,
            kernel_size, stride, padding, bias=bias)
        self.norm = norm
        if self.norm == 'batch':
            self.bn = torch.nn.BatchNorm2d(output_size)
        elif self.norm == 'instance':
            self.bn = torch.nn.InstanceNorm2d(output_size)
        self.activation = activation
        if self.activation == 'relu':
            self.act = torch.nn.ReLU(True)
        elif self.activation == 'prelu':
            self.act = torch.nn.PReLU()
        elif self.activation == 'lrelu':
            self.act = torch.nn.LeakyReLU(0.2, True)
        elif self.activation == 'tanh':
            self.act = torch.nn.Tanh()
        elif self.activation == 'sigmoid':
            self.act = torch.nn.Sigmoid()

    def forward(self, x):
        if self.norm is not None:
            out = self.bn(self.deconv(x))
        else:
            out = self.deconv(x)
        if self.activation is not None:
            return self.act(out)
        else:
            return out


class DownBlock(torch.nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, num_filter, kernel_size=8, stride=4, padding=2, bias
        =True, activation='prelu', norm=None):
        super(DownBlock, self).__init__()
        self.down_conv1 = ConvBlock(num_filter, num_filter, kernel_size,
            stride, padding, activation, norm=None)
        self.down_conv2 = DeconvBlock(num_filter, num_filter, kernel_size,
            stride, padding, activation, norm=None)
        self.down_conv3 = ConvBlock(num_filter, num_filter, kernel_size,
            stride, padding, activation, norm=None)

    def forward(self, x):
        l0 = self.down_conv1(x)
        h0 = self.down_conv2(l0)
        l1 = self.down_conv3(h0 - x)
        return l1 + l0


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'num_filter': 4}]

c = ( (C)^2 + (D)^2) - r^2

The discriminant is D = b² - 4ac 

If D < 0 → no solution, so no intersection.

t = [ -b ± sqrt(D) ]/(2a)

So in that case, then the minimal distance is the distance to the nearest endpoint, which we know is more than r.

    46	.. image:: https://img.shields.io/badge/Conventional%20Commits-1.0.<#sep#>import torch
from torchvision.transforms import *


class ConvBlock(torch.nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, input_size, output_size, kernel_size=3, stride=1,
        padding=1, bias=True, activation='prelu', norm=None):
        super(ConvBlock, self).__init__()
        self.conv = torch.nn.Conv2d(input_size, output_size, kernel_size,
            stride, padding, bias=bias)
        self.norm = norm
        if self.norm == 'batch':
            self.bn = torch.nn.BatchNorm2d(output_size)
        elif self.norm == 'instance':
            self.bn = torch.nn.InstanceNorm2d(output_size)
        self.activation = activation
        if self.activation == 'relu':
            self.act = torch.nn.ReLU(True)
        elif self.activation == 'prelu':
            self.act = torch.nn.PReLU()
        elif self.activation == 'lrelu':
            self.act = torch.nn.LeakyReLU(0.2, True)
        elif self.activation == 'tanh':
            self.act = torch.nn.Tanh()
        elif self.activation == 'sigmoid':
            self.act = torch.nn.Sigmoid()

    def forward(self, x):
        if self.norm is not None:
            out = self.bn(self.conv(x))
        else:
            out = self.conv(x)
        if self.activation is not None:
            return self.act(out)
        else:
            return out


class DeconvBlock(torch.nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, input_size, output_size, kernel_size=4, stride=2,
        padding=1, bias=True, activation='prelu', norm=None):
        super(DeconvBlock, self).__init__()
        self.deconv = torch.nn.ConvTranspose2d(input_size, output_size,
            kernel_size, stride, padding, bias=bias)
        self.norm = norm
        if self.norm == 'batch':
            self.bn = torch.nn.BatchNorm2d(output_size)
        elif self.norm == 'instance':
            self.bn = torch.nn.InstanceNorm2d(output_size)
        self.activation = activation
        if self.activation == 'relu':
            self.act = torch.nn.ReLU(True)
        elif self.activation == 'prelu':
            self.act = torch.nn.PReLU()
        elif self.activation == 'lrelu':
            self.act = torch.nn.LeakyReLU(0.2, True)
        elif self.activation == 'tanh':
            self.act = torch.nn.Tanh()
        elif self.activation == 'sigmoid':
            self.act = torch.nn.Sigmoid()

    def forward(self, x):
        if self.norm is not None:
            out = self.bn(self.deconv(x))
        else:
            out = self.deconv(x)
        if self.activation is not None:
            return self.act(out)
        else:
            return out


class D_DownBlock(torch.nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, num_filter, kernel_size=8, stride=4, padding=2,
        num_stages=1, bias=True, activation='prelu', norm=None):
        super(D_DownBlock, self).__init__()
        self.conv = ConvBlock(num_filter * num_stages, num_filter, 1, 1, 0,
            activation, norm=None)
        self.down_conv1 = ConvBlock(num_filter, num_filter, kernel_size,
            stride, padding, activation, norm=None)
        self.down_conv2 = DeconvBlock(num_filter, num_filter, kernel_size,
            stride, padding, activation, norm=None)
        self.down_conv3 = ConvBlock(num_filter, num_filter, kernel_size,
            stride, padding, activation, norm=None)

    def forward(self, x):
        x = self.conv(x)
        l0 = self.down_conv1(x)
        h0 = self.down_conv2(l0)
        l1 = self.down_conv3(h0 - x)
        return l1 + l0


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'num_filter': 4}]

    64	--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    65	
    66	:code:`sepal_ui` is a lib designed to create elegant python based dashboard in the `SEPAL environment <https://sepal.io/>`__. It is designed on top of the amazing `ipyvuetify <https://ipyvuetify.readthedocs.io/en/latest/introduction.html>`_ library and will help developer to easily create interface for their workflows. 
    67	By using this libraries, you'll ensure a robust and unified interface for your scripts and a easy and complete integration into the SEPAL dashboard of application.
    68	
    69	The full documentation is available `here <https://sepal-ui.readthedocs.io/en/latest/>`__ and a demo app can be launched on Heroku following this link: `<https://sepal-ui.herokuapp.com>`__.
    70	
    71	We are happy to receive feedback and we welcome any kind of contribution.

<#sep#>import math
import torch
from torchvision.transforms import *


class ConvBlock(torch.nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, input_size, output_size, kernel_size=3, stride=1,
        padding=1, bias=True, activation='prelu', norm=None):
        super(ConvBlock, self).__init__()
        self.conv = torch.nn.Conv2d(input_size, output_size, kernel_size,
            stride, padding, bias=bias)
        self.norm = norm
        if self.norm == 'batch':
            self.bn = torch.nn.BatchNorm2d(output_size)
        elif self.norm == 'instance':
            self.bn = torch.nn.InstanceNorm2d(output_size)
        self.activation = activation
        if self.activation == 'relu':
            self.act = torch.nn.ReLU(True)
        elif self.activation == 'prelu':
            self.act = torch.nn.PReLU()
        elif self.activation == 'lrelu':
            self.act = torch.nn.LeakyReLU(0.2, True)
        elif self.activation == 'tanh':
            self.act = torch.nn.Tanh()
        elif self.activation == 'sigmoid':
            self.act = torch.nn.Sigmoid()

    def forward(self, x):
        if self.norm is not None:
            out = self.bn(self.conv(x))
        else:
            out = self.conv(x)
        if self.activation is not None:
            return self.act(out)
        else:
            return out


class Upsampler(torch.nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, scale, n_feat, bn=False, act='prelu', bias=True):
        super(Upsampler, self).__init__()
        modules = []
        for _ in range(int(math.log(scale, 2))):
            modules.append(ConvBlock(n_feat, 4 * n_feat, 3, 1, 1, bias,
                activation=None, norm=None))
            modules.append(torch.nn.PixelShuffle(2))
            if bn:
                modules.append(torch.nn.BatchNorm2d(n_feat))
        self.up = torch.nn.Sequential(*modules)
        self.activation = act
        if self.activation == 'relu':
            self.act = torch.nn.ReLU(True)
        elif self.activation == 'prelu':
            self.act = torch.nn.PReLU()
        elif self.activation == 'lrelu':
            self.act = torch.nn.LeakyReLU(0.2, True)
        elif self.activation == 'tanh':
            self.act = torch.nn.Tanh()
        elif self.activation == 'sigmoid':
            self.act = torch.nn.Sigmoid()

    def forward(self, x):
        out = self.up(x)
        if self.activation is not None:
            out = self.act(out)
        return out


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'scale': 1.0, 'n_feat': 4}]

But given that x=7^{(p<#sep#> t in terms of how far along the line from P to get to the projection point:

the point on the line is P + t*VPQ = P + t*(dx, dy), where t = (CP • VPQ)/ (dx^2+ dy^2)

But since CP is from point P to point C, the vector CP is (C-P), so the original PQ is from P to Q, and the projection of C onto PQ is: 

the scalar t is as computed. 

So t can be:

t_ratio = ( (dCPx * dx + dCPy * dy ) ) / (dx^2 + dy^2).

Thus, if t_ratio <0: projection is before P, so closest is P. distance is CP_sqr_dist.

if t_ratio >1: projection is beyond Q; closest is Q, distance squared is CQ_sqr. 

else, the projection point is between PQ, distance squared is d_sq.

So compute the distance squared between C and PQ edge as either CP_sqr, CQ_sqr or d_sq based on t_ratio.

If this squared distance is ≤ r^2, then the minimal distance is within r, so the edge is near enough to the center, so the edge and the circle intersect (since the minimal distance is the closest, so if that's ≤ r, the circle touches or intersects the edge).

Therefore, the logic is:

If the edge's minimal distance (squared) is ≤ r², then return "intersected".

Hence, this approach allows us to avoid calculating roots.

Thus, the steps for an edge PQ (points P and Q):

Compute dx = Q.x - P.x 

dy = Q.y - P.y 

 dx_sqdy_sq = dx*dx + dy*dy 

 if dx_sqdy_sq is zero → Skip (edge length zero → non-existent)?

 else:

 compute dCPx = C.x - P.x → (C.x is x_coord of center here.)

 wait in this case, C is the center given by the query. 

 Wait variables: Center (x_centre, y_centre)

 P's coordinates are (px, py)

 Q's coordinates (qx, qy)

 then:

 dx = qx - px 

 dy = qy - py 

dCPx = x_centre - px, dCPy = y_centre - py.

 compute:

dot = dCPx * dx + dCPy * dy → 

t_ratio = dot / ( dx*dx + dy*dy )

 compute dCP_sq = dCPx*dCPx + dCPy*dCPy → CP squared distance.

 compute distance squared as:

if t_ratio <=0:

    dist_sq = dCP_sq → distance to P is compared against r squared.

elif t_ratio >=1:

    dist_sq = (qx - xc)^2 + (qy - yc)^2 → compute dxQ = qx-xc etc.

    dCQx = qx - xc → 

    dCQy = qy - yc → 

    dCQ_sq = dCQx*dCQx + dCQy*dCQy 

else:

    cross = dCPx * dy - dCPy * dx → 

    d_sq = (cross * cross) / ( dx*dx + dy*dy )

    dist_sq = d_sq 

so after determining dist_sq:

if (dist_sq <= r_sq) → return true for edge intersecting.

Thus, this can be done with all variables computed as squared to avoid square roots, and just comparing with the radius squared (entered early as such).

So this is a computationally feasible way.

Now, the edge check can be done in O(1) time per edge.

Thus, for each edge:

Conditional steps but all O(1).

This is manageable.

sepal_ui/scripts/gee<#sep#>import torch
from torchvision.transforms import *


class ConvBlock(torch.nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, input_size, output_size, kernel_size=3, stride=1,
        padding=1, bias=True, activation='prelu', norm=None):
        super(ConvBlock, self).__init__()
        self.conv = torch.nn.Conv2d(input_size, output_size, kernel_size,
            stride, padding, bias=bias)
        self.norm = norm
        if self.norm == 'batch':
            self.bn = torch.nn.BatchNorm2d(output_size)
        elif self.norm == 'instance':
            self.bn = torch.nn.InstanceNorm2d(output_size)
        self.activation = activation
        if self.activation == 'relu':
            self.act = torch.nn.ReLU(True)
        elif self.activation == 'prelu':
            self.act = torch.nn.PReLU()
        elif self.activation == 'lrelu':
            self.act = torch.nn.LeakyReLU(0.2, True)
        elif self.activation == 'tanh':
            self.act = torch.nn.Tanh()
        elif self.activation == 'sigmoid':
            self.act = torch.nn.Sigmoid()

    def forward(self, x):
        if self.norm is not None:
            out = self.bn(self.conv(x))
        else:
            out = self.conv(x)
        if self.activation is not None:
            return self.act(out)
        else:
            return out


class DeconvBlock(torch.nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, input_size, output_size, kernel_size=4, stride=2,
        padding=1, bias=True, activation='prelu', norm=None):
        super(DeconvBlock, self).__init__()
        self.deconv = torch.nn.ConvTranspose2d(input_size, output_size,
            kernel_size, stride, padding, bias=bias)
        self.norm = norm
        if self.norm == 'batch':
            self.bn = torch.nn.BatchNorm2d(output_size)
        elif self.norm == 'instance':
            self.bn = torch.nn.InstanceNorm2d(output_size)
        self.activation = activation
        if self.activation == 'relu':
            self.act = torch.nn.ReLU(True)
        elif self.activation == 'prelu':
            self.act = torch.nn.PReLU()
        elif self.activation == 'lrelu':
            self.act = torch.nn.LeakyReLU(0.2, True)
        elif self.activation == 'tanh':
            self.act = torch.nn.Tanh()
        elif self.activation == 'sigmoid':
            self.act = torch.nn.Sigmoid()

    def forward(self, x):
        if self.norm is not None:
            out = self.bn(self.deconv(x))
        else:
            out = self.deconv(x)
        if self.activation is not None:
            return self.act(out)
        else:
            return out


class UpBlock(torch.nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, num_filter, kernel_size=8, stride=4, padding=2, bias
        =True, activation='prelu', norm=None):
        super(UpBlock, self).__init__()
        self.up_conv1 = DeconvBlock(num_filter, num_filter, kernel_size,
            stride, padding, activation, norm=None)
        self.up_conv2 = ConvBlock(num_filter, num_filter, kernel_size,
            stride, padding, activation, norm=None)
        self.up_conv3 = DeconvBlock(num_filter, num_filter, kernel_size,
            stride, padding, activation, norm=None)

    def forward(self, x):
        h0 = self.up_conv1(x)
        l0 = self.up_conv2(h0)
        h1 = self.up_conv3(l0 - x)
        return h1 + h0


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'num_filter': 4}]

Wait, this suggests a mistake in the logic. Let me retrace. 

From the original equation: (7^{p-1} -1)/p =k². For p=5, this is (2401 -1)/5=2400/5=480. 480 is not a perfect square. However, according to the<#sep#>_vertex is not inside:

   compute if the distance_sq (based on projection) is ≤r_sq:

   if yes → return true for edge → thus polygon is in the list.

So the code steps for edge are:

 ... 

                 compute dCPx = x_centre - P.x

                        dCPy = y_centre - P.y 

                        dx = Q.x - P.x 

                        dy = Q.y - P.y 

                        dx_sqdy_sq = dx*dx + dy*dy 

                        dot = (dCPx)*dx + (dCPy)*dy 

                        t_ratio = dot/(dx_sqdy_sq )

                        if t_ratio <= 0:

                            // dist to P is already >r (has_vertex is false → so nothing), so don't need to check

                        elif t_ratio >=1:

                            // dist to Q: would have been in vertices check → also over sqrt(r_sq)

                        else: 

                            cross = dCPx * dy - dCPy * dx 

                            numerator = cross * cross 

                            dist_sq = numerator / dx_sqdy_sq 

                            if dist_sq <= r_sq → intersection → so polygon intersects, return true for this query.

                        else → no need to consider.

So finally, for the edge step, if any edge in the polygon satisfies the edge intersection condition, the polygon is counted.

Thus, for each edge, after calculating for the first step (has_vertex) is false, we can compute for each edge:

if any edge passes the edge test → then include this property.

Thus code steps.

Now, coding this efficiently.

This requires for each query and each property:

   first check the vertices (O(v) for each property's vertices).

if that passes: count it.

else, check each edge ( O(v) ), but for each edge compute distance.

Now, in the case of a polygon with v vertices, there are v edges (since it is a polygon, which is closed loop).

Wait:

Polygon with v vertices has v edges. For example, with 3 vertices (triangle), three edges.

Thus, for each polygon, edges are between vertex 0 and 1, 1 and 2, etc., and last to first (v-1 to 0).

Thus, in code, for each polygon:

for each i from 0 to v-1:

  P = polygon.vertices[i]

  Q = polygon.vertices[(i+1) % v]  // since for the last, next is 0.

But since it's a polygon provided as counter-clockwise, so the edges are correctly built.

But closed polygon, so the edges are formed between each consecutive pair.

This needs to be done carefully.

Now, in code structure.

First, for each property:

   store a list of vertices, and the precomputed area.

So in C++, the data might look like this:

struct Property {

   std::vector< std::pair<double, double> > vertices;

   double area;

};

Assuming the order is counter-clockwise.

Then, for pre-processing step:

   for each property, compute the area using the shoelace formula.

Thus:

   double area = 0.0;

   int v = # of points.

   for (int i = 0; i < v; ++i) {

       auto p = vertices[i];

       auto q = vertices[(i+1) % v];

       area += (p.first * q.second) - (q.first * p.second);

   }

   area = 0.5 * std::abs(area);

   store in property's area.

This is O(v) per property.

Now, for each query (xc, yc, r):

   r_sq = r * r.

   initialize total_area and list.

   for each property in the database (id, vertices, area):

      check if the property intersects the circle.

      // step 1: check vertices.

      bool intersected = false;

      for (auto &vert : property.vertices) {

          double dx = vert.first - xc;

          double dy = vert.second - yc;

          double dist_sq = dx*dx + dy*dy;

          if (dist_sq <= r_sq) {

              intersected = true; break;

          }

      }

      if (intersected) {

          add to total and list.

          continue; 

      }

      else {

          // step 2: check all edges

          intersected = false;

          for (int i=0; !intersected && i < v; i++) {

              auto P = property.vertices[i];

              auto Q = property.vertices[ (i+1) % v ] ;

              // compute edge PQ 

              double dx = Q.first - P.first; 

              double dy = Q.second - P.second;

              // compute parameters

              double dx_sqdy_sq = dx * dx + dy* dy;

              if (dx_sqdy_sq ==0) continue; // zero length?

              // compute variables:

              double p_x = P.first, p_y = P.second;

              // compute dCP = vector from P to C: (xc-p_x, yc - p_y). 

              double dCPx = xc - p_x;

              double dCPy = yc - p_y;

              // compute dot product between dCP and dx, dy (the vector PQ)

              double dot = dCPx * dx + dCPy * dy;

              // t_ratio = (dot) / dx_sqdy_sq 

              double t_ratio = dot / dx_sqdy_sq;

              if (t_ratio <=0 || t_ratio >= 1.0 ) {

                  // the closest is p or q, but those are already checked (as vertices are not in, so their distance must be > r_sq)

                  continue;

              }

              // else, compute cross.

              double cross = dCPx * dy - dCPy * dx;

              double numerator = cross * cross;

              double dist_sq_edge = numerator / dx_sqdy_sq;

              if ( dist_sq_edge <= r_sq ) {

                  intersected = true;

                  break;

              }

          }

      }

      if (intersected) {

          add to total and list.

      }

This is how it would be structured.

Now, considering the constraints:

In the worst case, this is O( n * ( v_vertices + v_edges) ) per query. Since v_vertices and v_edges are each up to 40 (because in a polygon, each edge is between vertices, so edges and vertices count are same). So per query, n * (40 + 40) * (some constants for steps) operations.

Assuming each step is a few mathematical operations, even 1e6 * 80 steps would be 8e7 operations which is manageable in C++.

Now, considering precision issues. 

The problem note says that "precision being used for solution checking is 1e-4".

So when comparing distances, using floating points, but since all operations are done with squared terms, possible to avoid sqrt.

But the problem says "touching or intersecting", so equality needs to be considered.

In the vertex distance check:

if the distance squared is exactly equal to r^2: it's considered as intersecting.

So the comparisons are correct.

Now, edge checking: the edge steps:

computer cross squared or etc. 

Now, possible corner cases:

- Edge exactly touches the circle: precisely when dist_sq_edge equals r squared → Yes.

- Edge is tangent to circle.

- vertices are on circle edge → which is covered by step1.

Now, code-wise, need to set the variables properly and loop correctly.

Now, code considerations:

In C++, using double for all computations. Since the input is double, that's needed.

Now, for large numbers of polygons (n up to 1e6), it's important to have them stored efficiently. Each polygon must be stored with its vertices and area. So:

A vector of Property structs where each struct contains the vertices vector and area. 

But even storing a vector of vertices for each polygon may be memory-intensive. For 1e6 polygons, each with, say 40 points, that's 40 million doubles stored → which is 320MB (since 8 bytes per double, ~80*1e6 bytes is 80 MB, 40 per would be 320MB). Maybe manageable, but can it?

Wait 40 elements per polygon * 1e6  = 4e7 elements. 4e7 * 8 bytes is 320,000,000 bytes (approx 320MB). That is acceptable for available memory (current coding contest memory limits are usually ~1-2 GB or higher). But whether the problem expects that.

Alternatively, maybe the constraints for n will be such that it's manageable, given n*q <=1e6.

But the code has to process.

Now, input handling:

The first line has the width and height as double, then n is integer.

Then n lines, each starts with v (number of vertices), followed by 2*v double numbers, representing the x,y coordinates of the vertices in counter-clockwise order.

import torch
from torch import nn as nn
from torch.nn import functional as F
from torch.nn import init as init
from torchvision.models import vgg as vgg
import torch.utils.data
from torch.utils import data as data
from torch import autograd as autograd


def reduce_loss(loss, reduction):
    """Reduce loss as specified.

    Args:
        loss (Tensor): Elementwise loss tensor.
        reduction (str): Options are 'none', 'mean' and 'sum'.

    Returns:
        Tensor: Reduced loss tensor.
    """
    reduction_enum = F._Reduction.get_enum(reduction)
    if reduction_enum == 0:
        return loss
    elif reduction_enum == 1:
        return loss.mean()
    else:
        return loss.sum()


def weight_reduce_loss(loss, weight=None, reduction='mean'):
    """Apply element-wise weight and reduce loss.

    Args:
        loss (Tensor): Element-wise loss.
        weight (Tensor): Element-wise weights. Default: None.
        reduction (str): Same as built-in losses of PyTorch. Options are
            'none', 'mean' and 'sum'. Default: 'mean'.

    Returns:
        Tensor: Loss values.
    """
    if weight is not None:
        assert weight.dim() == loss.dim()
        assert weight.size(1) == 1 or weight.size(1) == loss.size(1)
        loss = loss * weight
    if weight is None or reduction == 'sum':
        loss = reduce_loss(loss, reduction)
    elif reduction == 'mean':
        if weight.size(1) > 1:
            weight = weight.sum()
        else:
            weight = weight.sum() * loss.size(1)
        loss = loss.sum() / weight
    return loss


def weighted_loss(loss_func):
    """Create a weighted version of a given loss function.

    To use this decorator, the loss function must have the signature like
    `loss_func(pred, target, **kwargs)`. The function only needs to compute
    element-wise loss without any reduction. This decorator will add weight
    and reduction arguments to the function. The decorated function will have
    the signature like `loss_func(pred, target, weight=None, reduction='mean',
    **kwargs)`.

    :Example:

    >>> import torch
    >>> @weighted_loss
    >>> def l1_loss(pred, target):
    >>>     return (pred - target).abs()

    >>> pred = torch.Tensor([0, 2, 3])
    >>> target = torch.Tensor([1, 1, 1])
    >>> weight = torch.Tensor([1, 0, 1])

    >>> l1_loss(pred, target)
    tensor(1.3333)
    >>> l1_loss(pred, target, weight)
    tensor(1.5000)
    >>> l1_loss(pred, target, reduction='none')
    tensor([1., 1., 2.])
    >>> l1_loss(pred, target, weight, reduction='sum')
    tensor(3.)
    """

    @functools.wraps(loss_func)
    def wrapper(pred, target, weight=None, reduction='mean', **kwargs):
        loss = loss_func(pred, target, **kwargs)
        loss = weight_reduce_loss(loss, weight, reduction)
        return loss
    return wrapper


@weighted_loss
def charbonnier_loss(pred, target, eps=1e-12):
    return torch.sqrt((pred - target) ** 2 + eps)


class CharbonnierLoss(nn.Module):
    """Charbonnier loss (one variant of Robust L1Loss, a differentiable
    variant of L1Loss).

    Described in "Deep Laplacian Pyramid Networks for Fast and Accurate
        Super-Resolution".

    Args:
        loss_weight (float): Loss weight for L1 loss. Default: 1.0.
        reduction (str): Specifies the reduction to apply to the output.
            Supported choices are 'none' | 'mean' | 'sum'. Default: 'mean'.
        eps (float): A value used to control the curvature near zero.
            Default: 1e-12.
    """

    def __init__(self, loss_weight=1.0, reduction='mean', eps=1e-12):
        super(CharbonnierLoss, self).__init__()
        if reduction not in ['none', 'mean', 'sum']:
            raise ValueError(
                f'Unsupported reduction mode: {reduction}. Supported ones are: {_reduction_modes}'
                )
        self.loss_weight = loss_weight
        self.reduction = reduction
        self.eps = eps

    def forward(self, pred, target, weight=None, **kwargs):
        """
        Args:
            pred (Tensor): of shape (N, C, H, W). Predicted tensor.
            target (Tensor): of shape (N, C, H, W). Ground truth tensor.
            weight (Tensor, optional): of shape (N, C, H, W). Element-wise
                weights. Default: None.
        """
        return self.loss_weight * charbonnier_loss(pred, target, weight,
            eps=self.eps, reduction=self.reduction)


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {}]
